Title: Algorithmic Foundations of Deep Learning: Complexity-Theoretic Rates and a Characterization of Universal Approximation

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.26705v1

Published Time: Fri, 26 Jun 2026 00:37:13 GMT

Number of Pages: 86

Markdown Content:
# Algorithmic Foundations of Deep Learning: Complexity-Theoretic Rates and a Characterization of Universal Approximation 

## Anastasis Kratsios ∗ Simone Brugiapaglia † Bum Jun Kim ‡ Gregory Cousins §

## Haitz Sáez de Ocáriz Borde ¶

Abstract 

Feedforward neural network (NN) expressivity is typically studied by emulating optimal basis-expansion schemes. While powerful, this perspective is incomplete: it primarily captures complexity through regu-larity, and therefore does not distinguish intuitively simple and complicated objects with comparable regularity, such as the square-root function and a typical Brownian path. We address the first issue through a quantitative circuit-to-neural-network compilation theorem. The guiding message is that neural networks should be viewed not only as flexible basis functions, but also as models of computation . If a function f : [ −1, 1] d → R is computable to accuracy ε > 0 by a real-valued circuit over a prescribed elementary gate language, then f can be computed to comparable accuracy by an NN with explicit depth, width, and non-zero-parameter bounds controlled by the depth, width, gate count, and gate language of the original circuit. Thus, neural-network complexity is not governed by regularity alone, but also by the complexity of an elementary computation producing f .We then show that any definable feedforward NN model satisfying a natural parallelization condition, allowing possibly multivariate non-linearities such as attention or layer normalization, is a universal ap-proximator if and only if it contains a non-affine nonlinearity. This gives a general characterization of universality beyond the classical shallow multilayer-perceptron setting, where universality was character-ized in terms of the non-polynomiality of the componentwise activation. The scope of our theory is illustrated by deducing universal approximation guarantees for continu-ous functions, minimax-optimal approximation guarantees for Besov classes, logarithmic-error complexity for holomorphic functions, and by showing that NNs can emulate numerical algorithms such as Newton-Raphson root finding and power iteration without architecture-specific arguments. Its precision is illus-trated by shortest-path computation on k-vertex graphs: compiling the tropical dynamic-programming circuit yields NNs with O(log(1 /ε )) non-zero parameters, exponentially improving in ε−1 over the generic 

eO(ε−ck 2

) Lipschitz-approximation scale, for an absolute constant c > 0.

Keywords: Neural Network Approximation Theory, Universal Approximation, Circuit Complexity, Real-Valued Computation, Deep Learning Theory, O-Minimality, Neural Network Expressivity. 

# 1 The Gap: Classical Approximation Theory is not Rich Enough 

Neural-network approximation theory has been deeply influenced by classical ideas in constructive approx-imation theory organized around regularity: a target function is placed in a Hölder [204, 102], Sobolev, Besov [180, 148, 116], or holomorphic class [2], and networks are shown to emulate classical schemes such as splines [53, 54], wavelets [199, 32, 45], polynomials [115, 23] and their Stone-Weierstrass-Nachbin generaliza-tions [139, 179, 140, 156, 41], optimal linear combinations using optimally sampled function values [104, 55], sparsity via orthogonal polynomial approximators [4], holomorphy when available [36, 28, 144, 121], sparse basis expansions [85, 29, 30, 33, 159], or other specialized basis expansions [68, 120, 174, 108, 6, 167]. key advance showcasing the power of depth, namely approximation-rate accelerations that bypass the lower bounds of linear-width theory [152] and metric-entropy lower bounds 1 was pioneered in [203] and subsequently optimized in [173], by bringing into neural-network approximation the bit-encoding technique of [11] inspired by the adaptive two-scale cache-channel ideas of [170]. This innovation was decisive because it fundamentally relies on networks whose depth diverges with the target accuracy, unlike earlier O(1) -depth methods, which were essentially dictionary/basis-expansion schemes encoded into neural networks. Thus, bit-encoding showed that depth can exceed the maximal expressivity of any linear method; nevertheless, the arguments in [203, 173] remained rooted in classical Hölder-type approximation classes. While this viewpoint has proven indispensable for orienting questions of the form “what can neural networks represent?”, these tools remain fundamentally tied to a non-compositional approximation theory designed for basis-expansion methods. Although the limitations of non-compositional approximation theory have already been observed in [129, 154, 44], and although developments in “fully non-linear” approximation theory have begun [35, 150, 43], the resulting abstract approximation classes are still largely rooted in regularity. Figure 1: The Gap: In both panels, the red function is intuitively more difficult to describe, and hence should be harder to approximate, than its green counterpart. Nevertheless, the two functions in each panel have the same classical regularity: low regularity in a and high regularity in b. Thus, the regularity-based lens of classical constructive approximation theory, cf. [53, 52], or compressed sensing [4] (e.g., Besov or holomorphic), can detect neither the simplicity of the green curves relative to the hardness of the red curves, nor whether the approximators under study can exploit this distinction. In contrast, our complexity-theoretic lens (Theorem 3.2) predicts the gap in network size. Here, ε > 0 is the uniform approximation error over [0 , 1] .

Neural networks can easily approximate the elementary examples but naturally struggle with the more complicated ones; yet the conservative upper bounds of classical neural-network approximation theory fail to predict this gap. This raises the question: What structure, accessible through compositionality and depth, is invisible to the classical regularity-based lens? 

Rather than measuring the hardness of a function only through its smoothness or regularity class, e.g., Besov or holomorphic regularity, and then approximating it by truncating a basis expansion which is subse-quently emulated by a neural network, we measure hardness by the complexity of a non-recursive algorithm computing it . Such an algorithm is built from elementary computations, called gates, drawn from a fixed language. Examples include the R-algebra operations ×, k· for k ∈ R, and +, as well as more expressive operations such as division ÷, exponentiation exp , radicals √·, and tropical operations max {· , ·} . These operations capture many of the basic building blocks appearing in numerical analysis, scientific computing,

2and computer science, and recover the usual Boolean circuit viewpoint when restricted to the Boolean cube 

{0, 1}B . A function is thus “hard” precisely when it requires a large elementary recipe to compute uniformly from scratch. In short, we propose to augment the traditional purely analytic perspective on “hardness” by in-corporating combinatorial structures from classical theoretical computer science (TCS), e.g., [90, 113, 122, 91], and mathematical logic, e.g., [39]. Figure 2: The missing piece: algorithmic complexity. Classical approximation theory is black-box: it sees the regularity of the input-output map, but not the algorithmic structure producing it. Our framework interpolates between this regularity-based viewpoint and a white-box compilation of explicit algorithms. The grey-box theory forms the bridge: it converts circuit-level descriptions of a computation into neural-network approximation guarantees. 

Black -box setting: This recovers standard black-box approximation results for low-, moderate-, and high-regularity classes, including Theorems 4.1, 4.4, and 4.7. 

Grey-box setting: When the broad features of the algorithmic complexity are known, such as the growth rates of the circuit depth and number of gates as functions of the target accuracy, together with the elementary gate language used by the computation, Theorem 3.2 yields automatic neural-network complexity bounds. This is often the relevant regime for practical algorithms; see the lookup tables in Section 3.2.1, Corollary 5.1, and Figure 4. 

White-box setting: When the algorithm is explicit, Proposition 6.1 and Table 6 give a fine-grained gate-emulation route, used for numerical algorithms; e.g., Newton-Raphson root finding, power iteration, and ODE solvers (Section 5). 

## 1.1 Universal Computation: Qualitative and Quantitative Guarantees 

With this notion of algorithmic complexity in mind, our main quantitative result can be summarized as follows. If a continuous function f : [0 , 1] d → R can be computed uniformly to accuracy ε > 0 by a non-recursive algorithm in the above language, then any neural network architecture with a non-piecewise-linear nonlinearity can compute f to comparable accuracy using roughly the same number of neurons as the original algorithm has elementary gates. This is in the spirit of, and extends the scope of, the classical characterization in [153], which treated the shallow one-hidden-layer multilayer perceptron (MLP) model with a univariate componentwise nonlinearity; i.e., the shallow MLP model is a universal approximator if and only if its componentwise non-linearity is non-polynomial. Our informal universal computation theorem characterizes universality for general deep models, without imposing any MLP restriction. 

Informal Theorem 1.1 (Non-Affineness Characterizes Universal Approximation: cf. Theorem 3.1) . Any definable class of feedforward neural networks is universal in C([0 , 1] d, R), for every d ∈ N+, if and only if it contains a non-affine non-linearity. 

## 1.2 Complexity Guarantees for Neural Computation: Quantitative Guarantees 

Theorem 3.1, informally sketched in Informal Theorem 1.1, states that any computable function, in the relevant circuit model, can be computed by a neural network with a non-affine non-linearity. However, this qualitative statement does not quantify the size of the resulting network. It therefore leaves open the original hardness 

question posed in the thought experiment of Figure 1:

Figure 3: Informal Summary of the Quantitative Neural Compilation Theorem 3.2. Given a G-circuit computing f : [ −1, 1] d → R up to error ε > 0, we construct a neural network computing f up to error 2ε, with explicit depth, width, and non-zero-parameter bounds inherited from the circuit depth, size, and elementary-operation language. Each elementary gate is replaced by a neural emulator using the abstract surgery technique of Proposition 6.6. 

Our quantitative result (Theorem 3.2), summarized in Figure 3, answers Question (Q) by controlling the complexity of converting classical algorithms computing functions f : [ −1, 1] d → R, written using the usual elementary operations, into neural networks computing the same function up to a prescribed error. As in most real-valued computation, the target is not exact equality but approximation to accuracy ε > 0, typically uniformly over [−1, 1] d or in another relevant norm. 

## 1.3 Implications and Direct Consequences 

(i) A Unified Theory of Approximation and Computation. Our results recast neural approximation as neural computation: constructive approximation schemes, numerical algorithms, and TCS-style cir-

> 2

1 denotes the constant function x 7 → 1.

> 3

DPP stands for Bellman’s dynamic programming principle; cf. [13]. 

4cuits are all finite recipes built from elementary gates. The Quantitative Neural Compilation Theorem (Theorem 3.2) shows that, once such a recipe is written as a circuit, it can be converted into an ANN with explicit depth, width, and size bounds. (ii) A Phase Diagram of Neural Complexity Classes. This yields a phase diagram for neural com-plexity (Tables 3 and 4): the cost of representing a function is governed not only by its regularity class, but also by the complexity of the best circuit or algorithm computing it. Thus, computable functions may be far simpler than the worst-case elements of the same Besov or holomorphic class, provided that the operations and complexity of the algorithm computing the target are known (white-box) or can be estimated (grey-box). (iii) All Non-Affine Models are Minimax-Optimal Approximators. Our results provide a self-contained, unified guarantee: every definable feedforward architecture with at least one non-affine non-linearity achieves the standard minimax approximation rates for Besov (Theorem 4.4) and holomorphic classes (Theorem 4.7). These rates hold in Lpμ(X ), 1 ≤ p < ∞, for Ahlfors-regular measures on compact domains X ⊆ Rd, including domains of non-integer effective dimension (Theorem 4.1).                       

> Figure 4: Realistic network sizes for structured problems, beyond worst-case regularity. The all-pairs shortest paths (APSP) problem on a k-vertex graph defines a map f:Rk(k−1) /2→Rk(k−1) /2sending positive edge weights to the corresponding shortest-path distance matrix. Although this APSP map is 1-Lipschitz, classical best-approximation rates for the Lipschitz class, cf. [203, 173], lead to networks of size O(ε−ck 2

(iv) “Small” Neural Networks Can Solve Standard TCS and Numerical Analysis Problems. 

The same principle shows that generic feedforward architectures can emulate standard non-recursive numerical algorithms at essentially the cost of their pseudocode, once the elementary-operation language is fixed. This includes quadrature, ODE flow solvers, Newton-Raphson root finding, and power iteration from numerical analysis; see Propositions C.1, 5.5, 5.4, and 5.2. It also includes all-pairs shortest paths (APSP), illustrated in Figure 4; see Corollary 5.1. (v) Message to End-Users: Approximation Is Not the Differentiator. Under our hypotheses, universality, minimax-optimal approximation, and the ability to emulate many classical algorithms are generic con-sequences of non-affine feedforward computation. Thus approximation power is a baseline rather than a differentiator; architectural comparison should focus instead on inductive bias, symmetry, geometry, stability, scalability, and optimization behaviour. 51.4 Related Work 

Our results connect two largely independent branches of deep learning theory: one rooted in contemporary approximation theory, and one rooted in theoretical computer science (TCS). The novelty of our contribution is not the observation that depth, compositionality, or algorithmic structure matter in isolation; each of these themes has already played a central role in deep learning theory. Rather, the main conceptual contribution is to make circuit complexity and real-valued computability into approximation-theoretic objects: explicit computations are organized through primitive operation dictionaries and execution graphs, and then compiled into neural networks with quantitative, architecture-agnostic depth, width, and size guarantees. In this sense, the algorithmic viewpoint of TCS enters our framework not merely as analogy or motivation, but as the mechanism producing the approximation bounds. 

1.4.1 Compositional Sparsity: Contemporary Ideas in Approximation Theory 

Our perspective is complementary to the compositional sparsity ideas developed in the approximation-theoretic foundations of deep learning [129, 127, 166, 154, 26, 43]. In this line of work, the target is typically assumed to be well-described by a hierarchical composition of low-dimensional smooth, Lipschitz, Hölder, or Besov-type constituents; related depth benefits also arise for piecewise smooth functions with low-dimensional feature structure [148]. These results give a powerful regularity-and-composition-based explanation for why deep networks can overcome some of the limitations of shallow or linear approximation schemes. Our viewpoint differs in the object being compiled. Compositional sparsity studies targets whose input-output map factors through a favourable hidden compositional structure. Here, the structure is instead supplied by an explicit real-valued computation, built from a small and interpretable dictionary of elementary operations: addition, multiplication, division, radicals, exponentials, logarithms, maximization, and related primitives. This algorithmic viewpoint can yield smaller networks for realistic computations by avoiding worst-case overestimates for arbitrary constituent functions, even in one dimension; cf. Figures 1a and 1b. It also explains depth through the execution graph of the algorithm, gives interpretable neural emulators whose structure is inherited from the computation, and applies architecture-agnostically to a broad class of feedforward-type models, including modern MLPs and transformers with realistic normalization layers. Thus, the relevant complexity is not only the smoothness of the target map, but also the complexity of the best elementary computation producing it. 

1.4.2 Boolean Algorithmic Complexity Theory and Restricted Neural Network Models 

The connections between neural networks and algorithms date back to some of the earliest theoretical results on neural networks [125]. These connections have largely focused on the ability of neural networks to compute Boolean functions, i.e., maps f : {0, 1}Bin → { 0, 1}Bout , for some numbers of bits Bin , B out ∈ N+. This Boolean emphasis is natural: the associated complexity classes, such as NC 0, AC 0, and TC 0, are comparatively well-developed, and it is classical that a single threshold neuron computes the majority function when restricted to Boolean inputs {0, 1}B , for some B ∈ N+ [138, 162]. More generally, constant-depth polynomial-size MLPs with threshold gates realize Boolean threshold circuits, and hence capture TC 0-type computation; for example, parity is computed by a depth-two threshold network with linearly many hidden units [177, 147]. Analogous comparisons between smooth sigmoidal gates and Boolean threshold circuits were developed in [117, 118]. Thus, neural networks restricted to Boolean inputs can be strictly more powerful than AC 0, by the classical lower bounds separating AC 0 from parity and majority [74, 75]; related TC 0-type characterizations have also recently been developed for transformer architectures [126, 27]. The connections between recursive models of computation, e.g., Turing machines, and recursive neural-network models, e.g., recurrent neural networks (RNNs) [175, 176] and looped transformers [157, 31, 119], are also well studied. These results describe qualitative limits of neural-network computation in recursive or Boolean settings. By contrast, the language of circuit complexity theory, cf. [90, 91], is particularly well-suited to quantitative statements about depth, size, and gate structure. Our work follows this quantitative circuit-theoretic spirit, but moves from Boolean computation to real-valued uniform approximation, which is otherwise comparably underdeveloped. The connection between Boolean algorithms and neural networks is also central to the expressivity theory of graph neural networks. Standard message-passing GNNs are closely tied to the distinguishing power of the 1-dimensional Weisfeiler-Leman test, also known as colour refinement [202, 136], while higher-order architectures 6emulate stronger k-dimensional Weisfeiler–Leman tests and thereby yield finer expressivity hierarchies [136, 123, 25]. These works show that algorithmic tests can be used to compare neural architectures, but the algorithms being emulated remain discrete or graph-combinatorial. The present paper is complementary to this tradition: we use circuit complexity as a constructive approximation-theoretic language for real-valued computation. The primitive gates are arithmetic, radicals, exponentials, logarithms, maximization, and related real-valued operations, and our main theorems quantify how efficiently circuits built from these primitives can be compiled into broad feedforward neural architectures. In this sense, our framework is closer in spirit to continuous logic, where truth-values may be [0 , 1] -valued and structures are often metric or real-valued, than to the purely Boolean logical foundations of classical TCS; cf. [39, 67, 73]. We use this only as a conceptual analogy, but it helps explain why real-valued algorithmic primitives call for a complexity theory distinct from the Boolean case. 

1.4.3 Neural Algorithmic Reasoning 

A modern and largely empirical evolution of these ideas is neural algorithmic reasoning (NAR), cf. [188], which trains neural networks from algorithmic traces, i.e., intermediate states produced during the execution of an algorithm, with the aim of executing classical algorithms, improving interpretability and out-of-distribution generalization on combinatorial tasks, or transferring algorithmic reasoning to related tasks [200]. Repre-sentative examples include graph neural executors for breadth-first search, Bellman–Ford, Prim’s algorithm, dynamic graph connectivity, augmenting-path computations, and graph search [191, 189, 64, 63, 145]; CLRS-style neural processors trained across sorting, searching, dynamic-programming, graph, string, and geomet-ric algorithms [190, 84]; and structured neural executors based on priority queues, algorithmic pretraining, fixed-point reasoning, proximal-splitting emulation, multiple-solution supervision, and parallel algorithmic traces [86, 65, 66, 100, 107, 58]. These works provide compelling empirical evidence that trained neural architectures can emulate classical combinatorial algorithms. Theoretical support has begun to emerge; for example, in [56] it is shown that certain GNNs align with dynamic-programming principles. Such results are important, but remain architecture-specific and tied to particular algorithmic paradigms. By contrast, our goal is a circuit-level, architecture-agnostic compilation theorem. The closest theoretical result to our line of work is likely [103], which analyzes 

ReLU feedforward networks on digital computers within finite-precision machine learning; cf. [101]. There, inputs and outputs are discretized and networks may exploit additional bit precision, so the framework is intrinsically finite-state and does not apply directly to real-valued inputs and outputs. Our contribution is instead a real-valued circuit-to-network compilation theorem: primitive operation dictionaries and execution graphs yield quantitative, architecture-agnostic complexity bounds. 

1.4.4 Algorithmic Unrolling onto Neural Network Models 

Primarily in the numerical analysis and compressed sensing communities, NAR-adjacent ideas have also emerged independently, typically under the name of algorithmic unrolling . In this setting, a specialized or customized neural-network architecture is designed to approximately emulate a specific numerical-analytic al-gorithm, often one known to be efficient or near-optimal for a particular computational physics or engineering problem. A first prominent line unrolls sparse-coding, compressed-sensing, and inverse-problem algorithms: for instance, learned sparse-coding iterations are unrolled in [70]; LASSO, ISTA/FISTA-type, and related sparse-recovery procedures are unrolled or emulated by specialized networks in [133, 196, 201, 165]; and this viewpoint is further specialized to stable and accurate networks for compressed sensing and analysis-sparse inverse problems in [37, 142, 132, 131], or graph sampling algorithms [141]. A second line emulates proximal forward-backward splitting algorithms, in the sense of [38], by customized neural-operator models for solving specialized convex optimization problems [100]. A third line emulates fixed-point and iterative solution procedures for PDEs: this includes fixed-point schemes for semi-linear elliptic PDEs and their FB-SDE/2FBSDE counterparts [61, 62], learned iterative PDE solvers with convergence guarantees [83], neural or learned multigrid methods [69, 24, 72, 76], neural-operator corrections or warm-starts for relaxation and solvers [209, 111, 164, 98], and train-and-unroll neural-operator constructions [77]. 71.4.5 o-Minimal Definability: A Natural General Setting for Deep Learning 

Our main theorem applies to feedforward neural networks definable in an o-minimal expansion of the real field (R, ×, +, ≤), as defined below. This allows possibly multivariate and non-piecewise-affine non-linearities, including attention, SwiGLU , layer normalization, and standard bounded-domain positional encodings of the kind used in modern transformers. O-minimality provides a rigorous language for geometric and combinatorial “tameness” [49, 187, 183]; it has received significant attention in mathematical logic and number theory [151], and many useful structures are known to be o-minimal [198, 178, 19, 17, 18]. In learning and optimization, related tameness ideas appear in statistical learning theory [99], foreshadowed by Pfaffian VC-dimension bounds [92, 134], and in non-convex optimization through the Kurdyka-Łojasiewicz framework [109, 20, 22, 21]. Thus, o-minimal definability gives a broad but mathematically tame setting: many standard feedforward architectures used in practice, from classical MLPs and ResNets to transformers with realistic pooling and normalization layers, are definable in suitable o-minimal expansions of the real field and therefore fall within the scope of this paper. For a detailed catalogue of such examples, we refer the reader to the accompanying statistical paper [99], where it is shown that definable neural networks have finite sample complexity for regression and classification whenever the loss is definable. That work also contains an extensive list of definable architectures and loss functions, illustrating the breadth of the framework. 

## 1.5 Organization of the Paper 

The paper is organized as follows. In Section 1 we motivate the shift from regularity-based approximation theory to algorithmic complexity. Section 2 introduces ANNs, G-circuits, and the o-minimal definability framework. Section 3 states the main qualitative and quantitative results. Section 4 derives the unified minimax-approximation consequences for uniformly continuous, Besov, and holomorphic functions. Section 5 shows that ANNs emulate algorithms from TCS and numerical analysis, including shortest-path computation, power iteration, root finding, and structured ODE solvers. Section 6 explains the abstract-surgery mechanism underlying the quantitative compilation theorem. Section 7 concludes. 

# 2 Preliminaries 

An exhaustive list of the notation used in the paper can be found in Appendix F. We now recall the background and terminology needed to state our results. 

## 2.1 Model-Theoretic Geometry: o-Minimality 

We use o-minimal geometry to formalize the tameness of the non-linearities appearing in our networks. Throughout, an interval means one of (a, b ), (−∞ , a ), or (b, ∞), with a, b ∈ R, and each Rn is equipped with its usual (norm) topology. 

Definition 2.1 (o-Minimal Structure) . A structure expanding the real field (R, +, ×, 0, 1) is a collection S =(Sn)n∈N, where each Sn is a collection of subsets of Rn, satisfying the following conditions: (i) every algebraic subset of Rn belongs to Sn;(ii) each Sn is a Boolean sub-algebra of P(Rn);(iii) if A ∈ Sm and B ∈ Sn, then A × B ∈ Sm+n;(iv) if A ∈ Sn+m and π : Rn+m → Rn is the projection onto the first n coordinates, then p(A) ∈ Sn.The elements of Sn are called the definable subsets of Rn. The structure (R, +, ×, 0, 1, S) is o-minimal if and only if every definable subsets of R is a finite union of intervals and points. It follows inductively that the collection of definable sets is closed under any coordinate projections, equiv-alently under existential quantification. Since definable sets are also closed under complementation, they are likewise closed under universal quantification, by taking complements of coordinate projections of complements. See [184] for the standard foundational reference on o-minimality. 8Example 2.1. Examples, and non-examples, of o-minimal structures include: (i) The real field (R, +, ×, 0, 1) with no extra structure is o-minimal. The definable sets are precisely the semi-algebraic sets, since the standard ordering on R (considered as a relation on R2) is the projection of the algebraic set {(x, y, t ) : t2 = y − x} onto the first two coordinates. (ii) The structure (R, +, ×, 0, 1, Γsin( x)) is not o-minimal. If the graph Γsin( x) = {(x, y ) : y = sin( x)} is definable, then so too is the set {x ∈ R : sin( x) = 0 }, since it is the projection onto the first coordinate of the definable set Γsin( x) ∩ { (x, y ) : y = 0 }. This is an infinite union of isolated points, and so is strictly forbidden by the definition of o-minimality. (iii) Ran ,exp , the real field expanded by the graphs of all restricted analytic functions, and the full exponential function, is o-minimal, by [185] and by [186] using different techniques; extending the results of [198, 185]. In this setting, a well-behaved function is thought of as being tame when its graph is definable in some 

o-minimal expansion of the real field. 

Definition 2.2 (Definable map) . Let A ⊆ Rn. A map f : A → Rp is called definable in S if its graph 

Γf

> def.

= {(x, f (x)) ∈ Rn+p : x ∈ A} is a definable subset of Rn+p. We will say that a function f is definable when we mean that it is definable in some fixed o-minimal expansion S of the real field, and will say that f

is not definable when we mean that it cannot be definable in any o-minimal expansion of the real field. Observe that if f : A → Rp is definable for A ⊆ Rn, then so too are its domain A and range, since they are both projections of the graph f . Note that semi-algebraic functions are always definable. As shown in detail in [99], a broad class of modern feedforward deep-learning models used in practice is definable in suitable o-minimal expansions of the real field; for instance, in the Pfaffian closure of Ran, exp ,cf. [178]. We will repeatedly use the standard closure properties of definable maps: linear combinations, coordinate-wise concatenations, and compositions remain definable in the same o-minimal structure; cf. [40, Chapter 1.3]. Furthermore, it is easy to see that o-minimal structures are closed under reducts ; that is, if 

(R, +, ×, 0, 1, S) is an o-minimal structure expanding the real ordered field, and S′ ⊆ S is a subset which still generates a structure in the sense of Definition 2.1, then (R, +, ×, 0, 1, S′) is also necessarily o-minimal. For this reason, a given network that is definable in some o-minimal structure can also be assumed to be definable in a “smallest” o-minimal structure in which only the functions necessary to define the network are added to the standard real field. Lastly, we will make use of piecewise linear functions in this paper. We follow the convention that piecewise linear functions are not required to have polyhedral pieces over which they are linear. 

Definition 2.3 (Piecewise Linear) . A definable function f : Rd → RD is called piecewise linear if there exists a partition 3 {Π(p)}Pp=1 of Rd into definable sets such that, for each p ∈ [P ]+, there exist a D × d matrix A(p)

and a vector b(p) ∈ RD satisfying f (x) = A(p)x + b(p) for all x ∈ Π(p).

## 2.2 Deep Learning 

We fix a family of scalar-valued functions on Euclidean spaces of arbitrary finite dimension, which serve as the non-linearities in our models. We call this family the dictionary of non-linearities A ⊆ S∞ 

> n=1

C(Rn, R).4

The main content of our only assumption is that the dictionary A satisfies minimal tameness and closure conditions. The definability requirement excludes pathological activation functions that would not be used in practice, such as typical Brownian paths, or functions that are not statistically tame. For instance, such functions may fail to have finite γ-fat-shattering dimension, cf. [205, 172], and hence may fail to generalize in the uniform convergence model of learning theory [169]. It is also unclear whether they generalize in the PAC model, since their γ-graph dimension need not be finite [10]; moreover, they violate the broad sufficient condition for PAC learning in [99].   

> 3A pairwise disjoint cover.
> 4The non-linearities may depend on trainable parameters. Since our theorems do not require this additional structure, the parameterized setting is covered by adjoining the corresponding parameter instances to the dictionary. Likewise, vector-valued non-linearities reduce to the scalar-valued case by coordinate projection; hence we do not treat them separately.

9The remaining assumptions are satisfied by the standard feed-forward models we know of in practice, and mainly encode closure under concatenation. We prohibit piecewise linearity only for our main quantitative result, since the piecewise-linear case is effectively the well-understood ReLU -MLP case, cf. Lemma 6.7, under the o-minimal umbrella. The piecewise-linear case is treated in the qualitative Theorem 3.1.

We now define a (feedforward) neural network. We emphasize the dependence on the dictionary of non-linearities, which will ultimately be immaterial for our asymptotic computational results. 

Definition 2.5 (A-Neural Network) . Let A be a dictionary of non-linearities. For any din , d out ∈ N+, a map f : Rdin → Rdout is called an A-(feedforward) neural network, abbreviated ANN, if it admits a layerwise representation of the form

(2.1) where L ∈ N, d0 = din , d1, . . . , d L+1 ∈ N+, A(L+1) ∈ Rdout ×dL+1 , and, for every l = 0 , . . . , L , A(l) ∈ Rdl+1 ×dl

and b(l) ∈ Rdl+1 . For every l = 0 , . . . , L and every i ∈ [dl+1 ]+, the map ς(l,i ) : Rdl+1 → R belongs to A.We also regard every affine map x 7 → Ax + b as a depth-zero ANN; in particular, x 7 → x is an exact ANN. The feedforward structure above is uniform: its computational graph is multipartite, with possibly inactive connections between layers due to zeroed-out weights, whereas general circuits may have more complicated graph structure. The combination of multivariate non-linearities and zeroed-out weights allows a broad class of modern deep learning models to be captured by the definition of an ANN; cf. the extensive list, from practical transformers to MLPs, in the companion paper [99]. However, CNNs and GNNs impose additional constraints on their weight matrices and therefore form restricted subclasses; without further assumptions, one should not expect such restricted architecture classes to be universal at this level of generality. 

## 2.3 Non-Recursive Algorithms: Real-Valued Circuit Complexity 

We study neural networks as non-uniform real-valued models of computation by showing that they can approx-imately implement broad classes of circuits built from prescribed elementary gates. Throughout this section, complexity means real gate complexity: we count elementary operations, circuit depth, and later non-zero real parameters in the compiled neural network, but not bit complexity, parameter precision, conditioning, memory traffic, or training time. The circuit viewpoint includes finite Boolean [89], arithmetic [113, 114], probabilistic Boolean, and certain Pfaffian circuits [92, 137] as motivating special cases. We follow closely the presentation in [103, 110]. 10 Language Const. x 7 → ˜c + × | · | max Powers xℓ / radicals ℓ                                       

> √x1/x Representative computable objects
> Gk,c alg ✓✓✓××××Sparse and best polynomial approximators [4]; Bernstein approximators [115]; discrete FFT [135].
> Gk,c t-alg ✓✓✓✓✓××Spline approximators in Besov spaces [47, 54, 50]; tropical dynamic programming [91], including Floyd-Warshall [59, 197]; first-order splitting schemes for linear programs.
> Gk,c,r ⋆
> rt -alg ✓✓✓✓✓✓×Algebraic and tropical computations augmented by bounded-degree powers and radicals.
> Gk,c,r ⋆
> Rat ✓✓✓✓✓✓✓Finite-step Newton-Raphson and secant iterations on do-mains where the required divisions are well-defined [155]; matrix inversion via the adjugate formula on nonsingular matrices.

Table 2: Gate languages used in this paper. Green entries indicate primitive gates in the corresponding language, while red entries indicate operations not included as primitive gates. The final column lists representative objects and algorithms computable by circuits over the corresponding language, on the relevant domains; the list is illustrative rather than exhaustive. 

2.3.1 Language: Elementary Computations as Gates 

The complexity of a model or algorithm is measured relative to the language specifying which operations are treated as elementary, i.e., as having O(1) gate complexity. Thus, the expressive power of a computational language is largely determined by its gates : the operations it declares to be primitive. We denote the collection of admissible gates by G, and assume that G is a non-empty subset of S∞

> n=1

[Rn : R], where [Rn : R] denotes the set of all functions from Rn to R.The main gate languages used in this paper are the following. For k ∈ N+ and c > 0, the algebraic gate language Gk,c alg consists of constant gates x 7 → ˜c with |˜c| ≤ c, coordinate projections, and ˜k-ary addition and multiplication gates, for 1 ≤ ˜k ≤ k. The algebro-tropical gate language Gk,c t-alg augments Gk,c alg by the absolute-value gate and ˜k-ary maximization gates, for 1 ≤ ˜k ≤ k. Binary minimum is therefore also available, since 

min {x, y } = − max {− x, −y}; higher-arity minima are obtained analogously. For r⋆ ∈ N+, the radical algebro-tropical gate language Gk,c,r ⋆ 

> rt -alg

augments Gk,c t-alg by the power gates x 7 → xr , for 1 ≤ r ≤ r⋆, and the radical gates x 7 → x1/ℓ when this real-valued branch is defined, respectively x 7 → | x|1/ℓ when ℓ is even, for 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ r⋆.Finally, the rational-tropical gate language Gk,c,r ⋆ 

> Rat

augments Gk,c,r ⋆ 

> rt -alg

by the reciprocal gate x 7 → 1/x , used only on domains where the denominator is non-zero. Exponential and logarithmic gates are not part of these four core languages unless explicitly added in an extended gate language. The hierarchy of gate languages, and representative objects computable by circuits over these languages, is summarized in Table 2. Classical sparse polynomial approximator classes, such as best m-term approximation [4], are already computable in the algebraic language Gk,c alg . Pure dynamic-programming algorithms use only a proper subset of the algebro-tropical gates, cf. tropical complexity theory [91]. Piecewise-linear neural networks, such as ReLU -MLPs, also fit naturally into this hierarchy: they use affine operations together with piecewise-linear gates, and in particular implement binary maximization exactly by max {x, y } = x + ReLU( y − x). Binary multiplication is not primitive in such networks, but can be approximately implemented by standard logarithmic-depth constructions; cf. [149]. Having specified the basic units of computation , we now explain how these units are assembled into an algorithm . We adopt the non-uniform viewpoint of circuit complexity theory, where elementary operations are organized as a finite computational graph rather than generated recursively, as in uniform models of computation such as Turing machines. 

2.3.2 Directed Acyclic Graphs (DAGs) 

In what follows, we consider connected directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), where connected means connected after forgetting edge orientations. A DAG is a pair D = ( V, E ) of a (possibly infinite) non-empty set V ⊆ N

and directed edges E ⊆ { (v1, v 2) ∈ V 2 : v1̸ = v2} with no directed cycles. A finite sequence (vn)Nn=1 ⊆ V

satisfying (vn, v n+1 ) ∈ E for each n = 1 , . . . , N − 1 is called a directed path ; it is a cycle if N ≥ 2 and v1 = vN .11 A parent of a vertex v ∈ V is a vertex w ∈ V for which (w, v ) ∈ E, in this case, we say that v is the child of 

w. The set of parents of a vertex v is denoted by pa v

> def.

= {w ∈ V : ( w, v ) ∈ E} and the set of children of v is denoted by ch v

> def.

= {w ∈ V : ( v, w ) ∈ E}.Following standard circuit complexity theory, we will use DAGs to encode the computational graphs of algorithms, as in contemporary automatic-differentiation software such as TensorFlow [1] and PyTorch [146]. A vertex v ∈ V is called an input node if it has no parents; input nodes represent inputs, or components of vector-valued inputs, of the algorithm encoded by D. A vertex v ∈ V is called an output node if it has no children; output nodes represent outputs, or components of vector-valued outputs. The sets of input nodes and output nodes are thus, respectively, 

Vin 

> def.

= {v ∈ V : pa v = ∅} and Vout 

> def.

= {v ∈ V : ch v = ∅}.

All nodes in V which are not input nodes are called computation nodes ; these are precisely the points in the algorithm where a computation or readout occurs. The set of all computation nodes is 

Vcomp 

> def.

= {v ∈ V : pa v̸ = ∅} = V \ Vin .

Let d, D ∈ N+. We use DAG d,D to denote the set of connected DAGs with exactly d input nodes, exactly 

D output nodes, and finitely many computation nodes, #Vcomp < ∞. These DAGs will be used to encode circuits computing functions from Rd to RD .

12 3 Main Results 

## 3.1 Main Qualitative Result: Characterization of Universal Approximation 

We begin with our qualitative universality result, the formal version of Informal Theorem 1.1, before turning to quantitative circuit-to-network bounds below. The quantitative statements are cleanest for gate languages without reciprocal gates. 6 Informally, the definability hypotheses restrict the admissible non-linearities to tame operations admitting finite, though arbitrarily long, rule-based descriptions. In particular, they exclude typical Brownian paths, highly oscillatory activations of the kind considered in [205, 172], and unrestricted sine waves on unbounded intervals [94] as primitive non-linearities. In this tame setting, our main qualitative result shows that a definable feedforward model of the form of Definition 2.5 is a universal approximator if and only if its dictionary contains a non-affine non-linearity. 

Theorem 3.1 (Characterization of Universal Approximation) . Under Assumption 2.4 (i) and (ii), the follow-ing are equivalent: 1. Non-Affineness: A contains a non-affine non-linearity. 2. Universal Approximator: For any uniformly continuous f : [0 , 1] d → R and every ϵ > 0, there exists a ANN ˆfϵ : Rd → R satisfying 

sup 

> x∈[0 ,1] d

f (x) − ˆfϵ(x) < ϵ. (3.1) 

Proof. See Section E. Theorem 3.1 gives a definable analogue of the classical activation-function characterizations of universal approximation, replacing the shallow non-polynomial condition by the precise non-affine condition for the present definable feedforward model. This should be contrasted with the sufficient but not necessary conditions for universality developed in [95, 210]; which we now discuss before moving to our quantitative guarantee. 

3.1.1 Discussion: The Role of Definability 

The requirement that our networks are definable (within a fixed, o-minimal expansion of R), while the target function need not be, plays a precise structural role. The classes introduced in [210] are designed to cover a broad range of commonly used activation functions, including piecewise-smooth activations, smooth ReLU-type activations, and S-shaped activations. From the perspective of the present paper, however, these conditions are not tameness assumptions: they do not impose definability in an o-minimal structure, and therefore do not rule out artificial perturbations by sets that are not “tame” enough to be definable in an o-minimal setting. This is not a limitation of [210], whose aim is a broad class of sufficient conditions, but it explains how the o-minimality hypothesis is doing additional structural maintenance. Fix a shifted copy C ⊆ [−2, −1] of the classical Cantor set. Since every one-dimensional definable set in an o-minimal structure is a finite union of points and intervals, C is not definable in any o-minimal structure. Let 

ξ(u) def. 

= max 0, 1 − u + 32 dist( u, C),

where dist( u, C) def. 

= inf c∈C |u − c|. Since C is closed, the function ξ is continuous. Moreover, ξ is compactly supported and is not definable in any o-minimal structure; if it were definable then so would be C = [ −2, −1] ∩

ξ−1({0}), which is impossible. For each k ∈ N, consider the kink class A1,k of [208, page 2]. The non-definable ReLU k+1 variant 

σ1,k, bad (u) def. 

= ReLU( u)k+1 + ξ(u)  

> 6The reciprocal gate can introduce large quantitative overheads because of the singularity of 1/x at the origin. Quantitative results with reciprocal gates are also available, but are more technical; see Theorem 3.2.

13 belongs to A1,k , since near u = 0 it agrees with ReLU( u)k+1 and hence has the required k-th order kink. Next we consider the smooth ReLU-type class A2, defined through bounded S-shaped factors, cf. [210, page 3], this class contains the non-definable softplus variant 

σ2,bad (u) def. 

= log(1 + eu) + ξ(u)

which belongs to A2. Finally, the class A3 in [210, page 3], which contains the classical sigmoidal activation functions in [82], but it also contains the non-definable sigmoid variant

= eu

1 + eu + ξ(u).

Thus, the point is not that the broad classes of [210] are inadequate, but rather that definability serves a dif-ferent purpose: it rules out just enough pathological activation functions to obtain a simple characterization of universal approximation as being equivalent to being non-affine, while retaining real-world non-linearities; esp. those noted in [208]. Although statistical guarantees are outside our scope, ANNs with definable acti-vation functions were shown to enjoy finite sample-complexity bounds for classification and regression in [99, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2], thus further supporting the suitability of the o-minimal framework for deep learning. 

## 3.2 Main Quantitative Result: Circuit Complexity Controls ANN Complexity 

The main quantitative engine behind the paper is the following circuit-to-neural-network compilation theorem. We use the term compilation deliberately. At the local level, the proof replaces each elementary G-gate by a neural emulator . At the global level, however, the result gives a systematic translation of an entire algorithmic object written in one computational formalism, namely the source language of G-circuits, into an algorithmic object written in another, namely the target language of ANNs. Thus, the theorem compiles the full circuit, including its wiring, depth, width, and size information, into a neural network with explicit quantitative complexity bounds.

Remark 3.3 (Scope of the Quantitative Bounds) . The bounds in Theorem 3.2 are worst-case real-valued representation bounds. They count depth, width, and non-zero real parameters of the compiled ANN; they do not account for bit complexity, parameter precision, conditioning, memory traffic, or training time. The depth-dependent factors in the algebro-tropical, radical, and rational cases should therefore be read as part of the generic worst-case compilation cost. Before turning to the “white-box” analysis, cf. Figure 2c, where we directly estimate the complexity of neural networks computing functions whose explicit circuits are known, we first provide several “grey-box” lookup tables. These tables record the specialized consequences proved in Appendix A.5, and allow the reader to read off neural-network complexity bounds from two pieces of information: the growth rates of the underlying circuit and the language of elementary gates required to compute the target object. 

3.2.1 Grey-Box Lookup Tables: Neural-Network Complexity from Circuit Complexity 

Theorem 3.2 yields a general transfer principle: once a target function is computed by a G-circuit, it can be emulated by an ANN with explicit depth, width, and non-zero-parameter overheads. Here we record the specialized consequences for the complexity regimes most relevant to numerical analysis and construc-tive approximation theory; the corresponding derivations are given in Appendix A.5. Thus, in many regimes arising from constructive approximation and stable numer-ical algorithms, neural emulation preserves the dominant complexity scale.

Theorem 3.2 and Appendix A.5): Analogous setting as Table 3, but where the G-circuits computing f have polynomial depth ∆ ∈ O (κr ) with r > 0.Shading is as in Table 3, but now we use dark orange to emphasize changes in class, from polylogarithmic scaling to polynomial, or from polynomial to exponential. 

By contrast, Table 4 illustrates the cost of deep sequential computation. When the original circuit has polynomial depth, the emulator may move to a larger complexity class: polylogarithmic gate-count regimes may become polynomial in the induced non-zero-parameter count, and rational circuits may incur exponential-type factors through the term sO(κr ). This is not an artifact of the presentation; it reflects the role of circuit depth as a genuine measure of sequential computational hardness. Throughout both tables, circuit complexity is measured by gate count and width, while ANN complexity is measured by depth, width, and number of non-zero parameters. Other scaling laws, including non-polynomial regimes, follow from the same derivation and the bounds in Theorem 3.2. We do not tabulate them here, since in genuinely exponential regimes the resulting upper bounds are typically less informative. 

# 4 Implication I: Unified Approximation Theory for ANNs 

In this section, we show that the quantitative neural compilation theorem recovers, and simultaneously extends, standard approximation guarantees for deep networks. The point is simple: classical constructive approxima-16 tion schemes are algorithms. Once such a scheme is written as a circuit, Theorem 3.2 converts it into an 

ANN with comparable depth, width, and number of non-zero parameters. We organize the consequences according to the usual regularity scale studied in neural network theory and constructive approximation. At low regularity, we consider uniformly continuous functions on Ahlfors-regular supports, recovering universal-approximation-type guarantees [204, 171, 81]. At moderate regularity, we consider Besov classes Bsp,q (X ) on 

(ϵ, δ )-domains, with 1 ≤ p < ∞, 0 < q < ∞, and 0 < s < ∞, recovering the rates of nonlinear spline-wavelet approximation [53, 54]. At high regularity, we consider functions extending holomorphically to a Bernstein polyellipse, where sparse orthogonal-polynomial methods yield dimension-explicit logarithmic-error complex-ity [4]. These regimes correspond, up to polylogarithmic factors, to the constructive-approximation rows of Table 1; after neural compilation, they yield the ANN complexity bounds summarized in Table 5.                            

> Function Class Depth Width Non-Zero Parameters Reference
> Uniformly continuous functions finite finite finite Theorem 4.1 Besov space Bsp,q (X)O log 2(ε−1)O ε−d/ (sp )O ε−d/ (sp )log 2(ε−1)Theorem 4.4 Holomorphic class Hd,γ O dlog( γ−1) + dlog log( ε−1)O (γ−1log( ε−1)) 2dO (γ−1log( ε−1)) 2dTheorem 4.7

Table 5: Approximation guarantees obtained by compiling the constructive-approximation schemes from Table 1 into 

ANNs. The table records the resulting depth, width, and number of non-zero parameters required to achieve the error convention stated in the corresponding theorem. In the Lp rows, ε denotes the p-th power of the Lp error unless explicitly stated otherwise. 

Throughout this section, unless explicitly stated otherwise, Assumption 2.4 is in force. 

## 4.1 Low Regularity: Universal Approximation of Continuous Functions 

For any 0 < d ≤ n and any set A ⊆ Rn, we say that a Borel measure μ supported on A is d-Ahlfors regular if there exists a constant C ≥ 1 such that, for every x ∈ A and every 0 < r ≤ diam( A), we have 

C−1rd ≤ μ A ∩ B2(x, r ) ≤ Cr d. (4.1) Examples include Lebesgue measure, Riemannian volume measures when A is an embedded Riemannian submanifold of Rn, and also many self-similar measures on fractal sets; cf. [78]. We also recall that if a compact set A has box-covering dimension d, then for every η > 0 it can be covered by at most O(ρ−(d+η))

Euclidean balls of radius ρ as ρ ↓ 0; cf. [160, Chapter 9.1]. We also provide a sup-norm convergence guarantee, in the classical Stone-Weierstrass-Nachbin style of [82, 95, 102, 41, 16]. 

Theorem 4.1 (Universal Approximation on Ahlfors-Regular Supports) . Under Assumption 2.4, let X ⊆ [0 , 1] n

be compact of box-covering dimension 0 < d ≤ n, and let μ be a d-Ahlfors regular Borel measure on X . Let 

f : X → R be uniformly continuous with strictly increasing concave modulus of continuity ω. Then, for every 

ε > 0 it holds that: (i) For every 1 ≤ p < ∞, there exists an ANN Φf :ε : Rn → R satisfying 

Z

|f (x) − Φf :ε(x)|p dμ (x) ≤ ε. 

(ii) There exists an ANN Φf :ε : Rn → R satisfying 

sup

|f (x) − Φf :ε(x)| ≤ ε. 

When 1 ≤ p < ∞, there is a constant cX ,μ,p > 010 such that Φf :ε has depth O  log 2  1/(ε ω −1(cX ,μ,p ε1/p ))  ,width O  (ω−1(cX ,μ,p ε1/p )) −2d, and O  (ω−1(cX ,μ,p ε1/p )) −2d non-zero parameters.      

> 10 Depending only on p,μ(X), and the geometric constants of X.

17 Proof. See Section B. 

Remark 4.2 (Transparency vs. Optimality) . Since, below the Hölder scale, minimax rates for general ω-uniformly continuous functions are highly sensitive to the chosen modulus and are often less informative than the modulus dependence itself, we opt here for a simple and fully transparent proof of Theorem 4.1, rather than for a rate-optimized construction in this very general class. This argument makes explicit the dependence on every relevant parameter, including the modulus of continuity ω, the ambient dimension n,and the Ahlfors/box dimension d of the support of μ, that is, the dimension of the region “where the data lies”. In the next section, we turn to Besov classes, which contain Hölder and smooth classes as subclasses, and recover minimax-optimal rates in that regularity regime; see Theorem 4.4. In that setting, the resulting rates are directly comparable to the classical optimal rates. 

Remark 4.3 (Definable Supports) . If X is compact and definable in an o-minimal structure, then its box-counting dimension agrees with its o-minimal dimension [80]. If the target function f is also definable, then the minimal modulus of continuity is definable as well, and so specific properties of the ambient o-minimal structure can sharpen these results. For example, an o-minimal structure is polynomially bounded [184] if and only if, for any definable function g : R → R, there is some N ≥ 0 such that |g(t)| ≤ tN for all sufficiently large t > 0. In this case, the minimal modulus of continuity for definable f on a compact X is necessarily of Hölder type. The real field expanded by all restricted analytic functions gives an example of a polynomially bounded o-minimal structure. 

## 4.2 Moderate Regularity: Minimax-Optimal Rates for Besov Functions 

We consider the approximation theory of Hölder and smooth functions under their natural constructive approximation-theoretic umbrella of Besov spaces, since Besov regularity characterizes approximation by best 

N -term approximants at prescribed rates; cf. [52, Theorem 9.2]. In this sense, best N -term approximation rates are encoded by smoothness, equivalently, by the Besov space parameters. We briefly review these notions before presenting our approximation result. We emphasize the need to work on general domains, since Besov-regular functions arise naturally in PDE theory, Besov spaces subsume Sobolev spaces, and many PDE applications are posed on non-cubic domains. We restrict here to full-dimensional domains; Besov theory on fractal domains requires additional machinery and lies beyond the scope of this computation-and-approximation-theoretic paper.

## 4.3 High Regularity: Holomorphic Functions 

In this section, we consider the approximation of holomorphic functions of d variables by ANNs. We begin by recalling the class of holomorphic functions with “hidden anisotropy” used below. This class was studied in [2, 60] for ReLU networks. Functions of this type arise naturally in the study of parametric ODEs and PDEs; see, e.g., [4, Chapter 4] and [34]. For simplicity, we focus on functions with inputs in the hypercube 

[−1, 1] d and outputs in R. Extensions to functions of infinitely many variables with values in a Hilbert or Banach space, or to operator learning between Hilbert or Banach spaces, are possible but lie beyond the finite-dimensional scalar-valued setting considered here [2, 3, 5]. Define the d-dimensional Bernstein polyellipse with parameter ϱ = ( ϱi)di=1 ≥ 1d coordinate-wise as the Cartesian product

[−1, 1] d ⊆ B ϱ for every ϱ ≥ 1d and B1d = [ −1, 1] d. We will consider functions f : [ −1, 1] d → R that admit a holomorphic extension to a Bernstein polyellipse Bϱ.

Definition 4.5 (Holomorphic Extension) . We say that f : [ −1, 1] d → R admits a holomorphic extension to a Bernstein polyellipse Bϱ if there exists an open set O ⊆ Cd with Bϱ ⊆ O and a holomorphic function 

ef : O → C such that f = ef |[−1,1] d . Moreover, we define

If a function f admits a holomorphic extension to Bϱ, then the parameter ϱ measures its anisotropy; see, e.g., [4]. Intuitively, the larger the component ϱi, the more regular f is with respect to the i-th variable. We consider a class of such functions with hidden anisotropy , using a slightly simplified version of the setting in [2, 60]. 

Definition 4.6 (Hidden Anisotropy) . For d ∈ N+ and 0 < γ ≤ 1, the hidden anisotropy class Hd,γ consists of all functions f : [ −1, 1] d → R that admit a holomorphic extension to Bϱ for some ϱ > 1d such that

(4.3) The constraint (4.3) is chosen so that the best N -term approximation error of any function in Hd,γ with respect to Legendre polynomials decays exponentially; see Lemma B.2. Our main theorem shows that functions in Hd,γ can be accurately approximated by ANNs with moderate complexity. 

Theorem 4.7 (ANN Approximation of Holomorphic Functions) . Let d ∈ N+ and 0 < γ ≤ 1. Under Assumption 2.4, for every f ∈ H d,γ and every sufficiently small ε > 0, there exists an ANN Φε,f : Rd → R

such that

The proof of Theorem 4.7 is given in Appendix B.3. It is based on emulating sparse Legendre polynomial approximation, in the spirit of [3, Theorem 7.4], following ideas from [46, 48, 144]. 19 5 Implication II: ANNs Emulate TCS and Numerical Analysis Algo-rithms 

In this section, we illustrate the power of our main result, and its auxiliary tools, by showing that several well-studied classical algorithms admit efficient ANN emulators. These include high-dimensional ODE solvers, maximum-eigenvalue routines, root-finding algorithms, and shortest-path computations. We emphasize that these are real-valued representation results: the networks constructed below emulate explicit algorithmic computations, and the bounds concern depth, width, and non-zero parameters. Such structure-aware con-structions can be substantially sharper than bounds obtained by treating the same input-output maps only through classical uniform-continuity or Besov-type regularity. We emphasize that the main result extends well beyond the illustrations in this section: once an explicit computation is written as a circuit over one of our gate languages, Theorem 3.2 gives a systematic route to 

ANN emulation. We begin with a classical combinatorial example whose structure is invisible to a purely regularity-based approximation analysis. 

## 5.1 A Classical TCS Example: Shortest Paths on a Graph 

We first show how our main quantitative result, Theorem 3.2, directly implies the ability of neural networks to solve problems in classical TCS, which are not related to classical approximation theory in an obvious manner. Exponential Improvement over regularity-based bounds for combinatorial TCS problems. For reference, since the tropical circuit computing dist 1,2 is comprised only of the operations (1 , +, min {· , ·} ),the map dist 1,2 : (0 , ∞)|Ek | → [0 , ∞) is piecewise-linear and Lipschitz. More precisely, it is 1-Lipschitz with respect to the ℓ1 metric on the edge-weight vector, and (k −1) -Lipschitz with respect to the ℓ∞ metric. Indeed, 

dist 1,2(W ) = min γ:1 →2

We, where the minimum may be taken over simple paths, each using at most

If one ignores this tropical dynamic-programming structure and treats dist 1,2 only as a generic Lips-chitz function of |Ek| variables, then smoothness-only ReLU approximation theory gives only a curse-of-dimensionality scale; for instance, the very-deep modulus-of-continuity theory of [204] yields the generic scale 

eO(ε−| Ek |/2) = eO(ε−Θ( k2)) for Lipschitz functions, up to constants depending on the Lipschitz constant and the dimension. This generic smoothness-based scale is much larger than the complexity guaranteed by our circuit-to-network compilation in Corollary 5.1, which, for fixed k, gives an ANN with eO(log(1 /ε )) non-zero parameters. Thus, in this example, the regularity-only viewpoint gives a valid but highly non-adapted upper bound, whereas the tropical circuit description exposes the true algorithmic structure and is reminiscent of the recent breakthroughs in AI-assisted proofs for combinatorial problems, cf. [143]. 

## 5.2 Maximal Eigenvalue Solver 

Next, we consider a classical problem in numerical analysis: eigenvalue estimation. For concreteness, we restrict attention to positive-definite matrices satisfying a uniform ellipticity condition. Namely, for any 0 < δ ≤ ∆

and any d ∈ N+, let PD dδ, ∆(R) denote the set of d × d positive-definite matrices A whose smallest eigenvalue 

λmin (A) is bounded below by δ and whose largest eigenvalue λmax (A) is bounded above by ∆. Such matrices arise naturally in elliptic PDEs; cf. [105, Chapter 14], and in fully nonlinear BSDEs; see, e.g., [62]. We then restrict attention further to the subset of matrices A ∈ PD dδ, ∆(R) with a spectral gap of at least γ > 0, i.e., such that 

γ ≤ λmax (A) − λmax −1(A), (5.2) where λmax −1(A) denotes the second-largest eigenvalue of A (necessarily unique in this case). The collection of all such matrices is denoted by PD dδ,γ, ∆(R). We note that matrices with spectral gaps arise naturally in the study of mixing times for Markov processes [8, 7], with applications ranging from learning theory with non-i.i.d. data [192, 112] to differential privacy [207]. We emulate the classical power iteration algorithm; cf. Algorithm 2 and [130]. Its convergence is exponentially fast provided the initialization has non-zero overlap with the top eigenspace; cf. [9, Theorem 7.6].

## 5.3 Root Finding 

As an example of neural networks implementing root-finding procedures, we consider the computation of radicals. Indeed, a radical amounts to solving 

xℓ = y

for a given input x ≥ 0 and a fixed integer ℓ ∈ N+. Although this construction appears inside the proof of Proposition 6.1, we isolate it here as an illustrative example of how an ANN can emulate a classical numerical routine. The proof of the next result shows that ANNs can approximately implement the Newton iterations in Algorithm 3, using only simpler algebraic gates as building blocks. Moreover, Algorithm 3 calls Algorithm 7 as a subroutine, so the resulting “expert/agent” network reuses the previously constructed inversion network as a sub-network.

## 5.4 High-Dimensional Structured ODE Solvers Emulation 

As we know from the theory of (non-linear) widths [35, 150, 174] and entropy numbers [96, 97, 106, 124], the minimax-optimal worst-case complexity for approximating arbitrary Cs, 1([0 , 1] d+1 , Rd) functions, measured in the uniform ℓ2-norm, scales componentwise as in the scalar case, up to constants depending on d; this can be discouraging in high dimensions if we cannot guarantee that the target function has any real smoothness (e.g., if only s = 1 ). As an illustration of how our main result allows for such finer structure to be easily incorporated, we consider the following flow map 

Flow α,β : Rd × [0 , 1] → Rd,

(x, t ) 7 → Xxt , (5.3) where for any x ∈ Rd, we use (Xxt )t∈[0 ,1] to denote the unique absolutely continuous solution to the following ODE ∂t Xt = ( α(t)A) Xt + ( β(t)B),X0 = x, (5.4) where α, β : R → R are Lipschitz, A ∈ Rd×d, and B ∈ Rd.We emphasize that the main point here is the stylized structure, which cannot be detected by the “un-structured” minimax approximation theorems for classical Besov/ Cs, 1-type classes. Applied componentwise to vector-valued functions and measured in the uniform ℓ2-norm, such results only yield dimension-dependent rates governed by the ambient input dimension; which only yield rates of order O(ε−d/s ); cf. [51]. 

Proposition 5.5 (Flow-Map Emulation for Scalar-Modulated Linear Dynamics) . Let A ∈ Rd×d, B ∈ Rd,and let α, β : [0 , 1] → R be Lipschitz. Consider the vector-valued flow map Flow α,β , cf. (5.3) , associated to the ODE (5.4) . Then Flow α,β |[0 ,1] d×[0 ,1] ∈ C1,1([0 , 1] d+1 , Rd). Under Assumption 2.4, for every ε > 0, there exists an ANN [Flow α,β,ε : Rd+1 → Rd such that

## 6.2 Step 2: Surgery 

We now present the main technical vehicle of our approach, building on [103, 110], which we term abstract surgery . This mechanism combines the individual approximation guarantees for each gate in any considered class, e.g., those enumerated in Table 6, into a single ANN, and is presented in full generality both to expose its core structure and to facilitate future applications. The procedure consists of three steps (cf. Figures 6b and 8): (1) construct a dictionary of approximate 

ANN gate emulators, one for each gate in G; (2) rewrite the target circuit into an equivalent multipartite form aligned with the graph structure of an ANN; and (3) iteratively replace each gate by its ANN emulator, padding layers appropriately to ensure alignment. The key difficulty in (3) is to do so in a manner that               

> 12 If g:Rk→Rand dom( g)⊆Rkthen the source of gis Rkeven if its domain may be strictly smaller.

24 prevents the successive composition of operations of progressively lower regularity from causing a complexity explosion. The abstract surgery lemma below executes this procedure, given a dictionary of elementary gate emulators. In short, abstract surgery reduces the ANN approximation problem to: (1) bounding the complexity of approximately emulating each gate by an ANN, and (2) exhibiting a G-circuit computing the target function. To quantify the hardness of this problem in terms of the depth, width, and gate count of the circuit, we require two additional pieces of metric information, one regularity-theoretic and one bornological. The first is captured by G-propagators, which measure how much a given gate can stretch a standard cube, modulo points of ill-definition. 

Definition 6.2 (G-Propagator) . Let G be a family of gates. A G-propagator is a map ΦG : [0 , ∞) → [0 , ∞]

such that: for every G ∋ g : Rdg ⊇ dom( g) → R, each M > 0,

g([ −M, M ]dg ∩ dom( g)) ⊆ [−ΦG(M ), ΦG(M )] .

The second missing piece of information, quantified by G-regulators, describes the worst-case modulus of continuity that any gate in G can exhibit when restricted to any given cube; omitting points where that gate is ill-defined. 

Definition 6.3 (G-Regulators) . Let G be a family of gates. A G-regulator is a map RG : [0 , ∞) →

C([0 , ∞), [0 , ∞)) such that RG(M ) is a concave modulus of continuity and: for every G ∋ g : Rdg ⊇ dom( g) →

R, each M > 0, and every x, ˜x ∈ [−M, M ]dg ∩ dom( g),

|g(x) − g(˜ x)| ≤ R G(M )( ∥x − ˜x∥∞).

In addition, we need one last piece of information, which our analysis to date has estimated for the class of gates considered herein. Namely, we need the hardness of approximating any individual gate by an ANN. In this paper, we consider general classes of activation dictionaries and, accordingly, provide worst-case bounds; however, one can easily imagine obtaining specialized bounds by directly applying abstract surgery with specialized classes of activation dictionaries A.

Definition 6.4 ((G, A)-Hardness) . Let G be a family of gates and A be an activation dictionary. The (G, A)-hardness is a map HG,A : [0 , ∞)2 → N3+ such that: for every G ∋ g : Rdg ⊇ dom( g) → R, each side-length 

M > 0, and every uniform approximation error ε > 0, writing HG,A(M, ε ) = (∆ M,ε , ΥM,ε , N M,ε ), there exists an ANN ˆgε,M : Rdg → R of depth ∆M,ε , width ΥM,ε , and with no more than NM,ε non-zero parameters satisfying 

sup  

> x∈dom( g)∩[−M,M ]dg

|g(x) − ˆgε,M (x)| < ε. 

Note that, the complexity bounds in Table 6, which make up the bulk of our analysis, are precisely estimates on the (G, A)-Hardness. We are now ready to deduce our main result, Theorem 3.2. Its proof relies on the G-regulator and G-propagator bounds summarized in Table 7, together with the size of the ANNs required to approximate the gates defining each class, as summarized in Table 6. All results are stated for inputs in the cube [0 , 1] d, with 

M ≥ 1 and, when relevant, m > 0. For rational-tropical gates, we restrict attention to functions computed by “non-singular” gates, meaning that no forbidden m-composition is performed.

## 6.3 Key Technical Detail: Where o-Minimality Matters 

There are two critical places where definability in an o-minimal structure plays a crucial technical role. The first, and most important, is that any non-piecewise linear function which is definable in an o-minimal structure must have at least one “good point” at which the function is twice continuously differentiable and has non-degenerate Hessian. Surprisingly, as foreshadowed by the more restrictive technical condition in [95], this is already enough for us to build networks that emulate multiplication and, once coupled with the affine layers of an ANN, virtually every other basic gate arising from it. Under o-minimality and continuity, the failure of piecewise linearity of a function is equivalent to the existence of a “good point”; by which we mean a point at which the function is twice continuously differentiable and at which one of its second-order partial derivatives does not vanish. This is reminiscent of the technical condition in [95, Theorem 3.2] and the A3 class in [210, page 3]. Interestingly, in the o-minimal setting there are only two possibilities: piecewise linearity and the existence of a “good point”. 26 Lemma 6.7 (Characterization of Piecewise Linearity in o-Minimal Structures) . Let d, D ∈ N+, and let 

f : Rd → RD be continuous and definable in an o-minimal structure. Then, the following are equivalent: (i) Non-Piecewise Linearity: f is not piecewise linear, (ii) There exists a point x ∈ Rd and a radius r > 0 such that for every x′ ∈ B(x, r ) and every r′ > 0 with 

B(x′, r ′) ⊆ B(x, r ), f |B(x′,r ′) is not affine, (iii) Good Point: There exists a “good point” x⋆ ∈ Rd at which f is twice continuously differentiable and at which one of its second-order partial derivatives does not vanish. Proof. See Section A. 

# 7 Conclusion 

This paper develops a complexity-theoretic view of neural-network approximation. Its main point is that neural-network expressivity is not governed only by the regularity of the target function, but also by the complexity of the best elementary algorithm computing it. Theorem 3.1 gives the qualitative statement: within the definable feedforward setting, universal approximation is equivalent to the presence of a non-affine nonlinearity. Thus universality is generic, stable under composition, and not an artifact of a particular shallow architecture. Theorem 3.2 gives the quantitative statement: every G-circuit computing a target map can be compiled into an ANN with explicit depth, width, and size bounds inherited from the circuit. Consequently, standard approximation schemes, numerical algorithms, and TCS-style dynamic programs all become neural-network constructions once written as elementary circuits. This recovers minimax-optimal approximation guarantees for classical regularity classes, and also shows that ANNs can compute algorithms from numerical analysis and combinatorial optimization at comparable worst-case complexity. 

## 7.1 Future Work 

On the theoretical side, a natural new question is to determine whether ANNs are only efficient emulators of classical real-valued circuits, or whether their richer dictionaries of trainable nonlinearities, shared modules, attention, pooling, and normalization can yield provable complexity separations. This would require lower bounds for restricted circuit languages, for which may be possible by using the recent theory of Lipschitz widths [150] or their manifold width counterpart [35]. On the empirical side, it would be interesting to use our results to construct a “ neural network compiler ”building on Theorem 3.2. Such a meta-algorithm would accept pseudo-code as input, akin to the ones peppered throughout our manuscript and emulated by the neural networks we constructed in the case-by-case analysis of Proposition 6.1, and would return a concrete neural network which certifiably computes that algorithm. We expect that the framework developed herein would allow this, since we explicitly constructed most of those neural networks in the proofs. 

# A Proof of Theorem 3.2 

We begin with the proof of the technical tools on which our first few steps build. 

Proof of Lemma 6.7. Suppose that f is piecewise linear. Let Rd = ∪Pp=1 Π(p) be a finite partition such that f

is affine on each Π(p). Let B(x, r ) be an arbitrary open ball. Then Π(p) ∩ B(x, r ) has non-empty interior for at least one p, and so we may always find a sub-ball B(x′, r ′) ⊆ B(x, r ) such that the restriction of f to B(x′, r ′)

is affine. Thus (ii) fails; equivalently, by contraposition, (ii) implies (i). Conversely, suppose that f is not piecewise linear. By o-minimal cell decomposition, there is a finite partition Rd = U1 ∪ . . . ∪ Uk such that fi = f |Ui is C2, and such that cells of dimension d are open. Without loss of generality, there is at least one cell Ui of dimension d and indices j ∈ [D]+ and i1, i 2 ∈ [d]+ such that 

∂i1 ∂i2 (fi)j is not identically 0; if not, then f would be affine on each full-dimensional cell, and, by continuity 27 and induction on the dimension, piecewise linear on Rd. Consider the set O = {x ∈ Ui : ∂i1 ∂i2 (fi)j (x)̸ = 0 }.Since (fi)j is C2, ∂i1 ∂i2 (fi)j : Ui → R is continuous, and so O is non-empty and open. Therefore, there is some ball B(x, r ) ⊆ O such that, on B(x, r ), ∂i1 ∂i2 (fi)j is nowhere 0. In particular, fi (and hence f ) is not affine on any sub-ball of B(x, r ); thus (i) implies (ii). It remains to show that (ii) and (iii) are equivalent. Since every open ball is definable, [40, Theorem 6.7] implies that there exists a finite number of definable C2-submanifolds M1, . . . , Mp ⊆ B(x, r ) on which 

f |Mk is twice continuously differentiable. Now, since there are finitely many such definable manifolds covering 

B(x, r ), there must exist some p′ ∈ [p] such that Mp′

has full dimension ( dim( Mp′

) = d). Consequently, there must exist some open ball B(x′, r ′) ⊆ M p′

; whence, f |B(x′,r ′) is C2. If for every j ∈ [D]+ all second partials 

∂i1 ∂i2 fj vanished identically on B(x′, r ′), then each component fj would be affine on B(x′, r ′), hence f would be affine on B(x′, r ′), contradicting (ii). Therefore, there exist j ∈ [D]+ and i1, i 2 ∈ [d]+ such that ∂i1 ∂i2 fj

is not identically zero on B(x′, r ′), and thus there exists x0 ∈ B(x′, r ′) with ∂i1 ∂i2 fj (x0)̸ = 0 , proving (iii). Conversely, if (iii) holds, then by continuity of the relevant second-order partial derivative there exists a ball 

B(x⋆, r ) on which it is nowhere zero. Hence f cannot be affine on any sub-ball of B(x⋆, r ).

## A.1 Proof of Step 1: Proposition 6.1 

We now prove Proposition 6.1. The proof proceeds via a set of case-by-case arguments, wherein each gate is encoded into a general ANN whose activation dictionary A contains at least one non-piecewise affine function. We begin with the simulation of elementary building blocks and simpler gates (e.g., + and ×), which are in turn called as sub-networks, simulating sub-algorithms, within more complex procedures encoded by networks computing the more sophisticated gates (e.g., √· or 1 

> ·

) and sub-networks (e.g., k-ary maximization, tensorized splines, or sparse polynomials).

It remains to tally the complexity. The tree has ⌈log 2(k)⌉ levels of multiplications, so the depth is at most 

3⌈log 2(k)⌉ for the multiplication blocks, plus 1 affine layer for the input rescaling and 1 affine layer for the output rescaling. The maximal parallelism occurs at the first level, which contains ⌈k/ 2⌉ multiplications in parallel, hence the stated width bound. Finally, there are k −1 multiplication blocks (after pruning the padded 

1 leaves), each contributing at most 4(2 ∥x⋆∥0 +5)+11 non-zero parameters, plus k for the affine input rescaling, plus at most 2k + 2 for wiring and the final affine output scaling.

As a consequence, we may approximate the exponential map by emulating the Picard iteration for the one-dimensional ODE u′(x) = u(x), whose Picard operator is given by u(·) 7 → 1 + R x 

> 0

u(t) dt . We remark that the iterates of this map, evaluated first at the constant function u(t) = 1 for all t, are given exactly, and conveniently, by the truncations of the Taylor series expansion of ex.We mention this because the Picard iteration fundamentally uses depth, while, in contrast, an Euler-type approximation such as ex ≈ (1 + x/k )k does not. Moreover, we now have enough elementary gates emulated to efficiently encode the convenient truncated Taylor-series representation of these iterates.

We are now prepared to implement the radical operation. This will allow us to unlock several approximate algorithms, especially the ability to approximately emulate the tropical operations. We show that ANNs can approximately compute the Newton iterations for radicals given in Algorithm 3. This procedure calls Algorithm 7 as a subroutine; equivalently, the resulting “expert/agent” network incorporates the previously constructed inversion network as a sub-network.

A.2.6 Tropical Operations 

We begin by emulating the binary maximum, as the k-ary maximum may be derived from it by the usual tree-based iteration ( N C 0-type tropical circuit). Likewise, the binary minimum is obtained by conjugating the binary maximum with a negation, which can be computed by an ANN. In order to obtain the maximum we must first emulate the absolute value operation. This too can be obtained via the elementary identity: for every x ∈ R

|x| = √x2. (A.25) Next, we exhibit an ANN which approximately computes the left-hand side of (A.25) by calling the radical and squaring “expert/agent” networks as sub-routines; this is performed in the following Algorithm 8. 42 Algorithm 8: ANN Absolute Value via Squaring and Radicals 

Input: x ∈ [−M, M ], parameters M ≥ 2, 0 < ε < 1

Output: ba ≈ | x| 

> 1

u def. 

= Square (x) ; // Square is Proposition A.3  

> 2

ba def. 

= Radical (u; ℓ = 2) ; // Radical is Algorithm 3  

> 3

return ba;Note that, we have previously shown that all of the operations in (A.25) are approximately (and efficiently) emulatable by ANNs. Thus, so must the absolute value be; and consequently so must the binary maximum (respectively, minimum) be.
