Corollary A.7 (AN N Emulation of the Binary Minimum min {· , ·} ). For every 0 < ε < 1 and M ≥ 2 there exists an ANN Min M :ε : R2 → R satisfying

> |x|≤ M, |y|≤ M

Since min {x, y } = − max {− x, −y}, the claim follows immediately from Proposition A.11. 

Remark A.12 (A Curious Contrast with the ReLU -MLP arguments) . The reader familiar with the usual proofs of universal approximation specialized to the highly particular structure of the ReLU -MLP model, cf. [203, 210, 149], which is used less and less in real-world AI applications, will notice a curious feature of our arguments to date. Namely, in the general non-flat o-minimal setting ANNs easily emulate squares but have trouble emulating the absolute value function; this is exactly the opposite of what happens in the specialized 

ReLU -MLP model where one has to make use of Telgarsky’s sawtooth construction, cf. [181], to emulate the quadratic monomial. 

A.2.7 Tensorized B-Splines and Bump Functions of Moderate Regularity 

There are at least two polynomial splines which play core roles in several central results in approximation theory and numerical analysis. These are moderate-regularity bump functions and cardinal B-splines. We now obtain ANN computations of these rudimentary objects, by stringing together our previous basic building blocks; in other words we are encoding basic Gk 

> alg

circuits computing them; for suitable but small k.

## A.3 Proof of Step 2: Proposition 6.6 

The remainder of this section is devoted to proving our abstract surgery theorem; Proposition 6.6. 48 A.3.1 Step 1: Circuit Canonicalization: Matching the Computational Graphs of a ANN 

A general G-circuit need not have a multi-partite computational graph structure (resolved in Lemma A.15); moreover, the second issue is that the nodes on the same part may require different complexity to emulate by a neural network (resolved in Lemma A.16). Once both these issues are shown to be resolvable in a “tame manner” then all the approximate gate emulation computations we have made, summarized in Table 6 will directly imply our main universal computation theorem : Theorem 3.1. Computation Nodes Latent Variables 

> Variables

-Circuit   

> (a) The original G-circuit. Computation Nodes Latent Variables
> Variables

Canonicalized -Circuit (b) The canonicalized G⋆-circuit. 

Figure 6: Canonicalization of the G-Circuit in Figure 6a: The circuit computing the same function f is endowed with a multi-partite structure, thereby mimicking the “uniform” structure of an ANN. This is achieved by first aligning computation nodes according to their distance from the input variables, and then subdividing edges which “hop across” levels of the circuit by inserting new nodes labelled with identity gates. 

Without loss of generality, we only consider the case of a one-dimensional output, as one may directly obtain the general multi-dimensional output case by parallelization; which is no issue by Assumption 2.4 (ii).

with Vout = 1 of has depth L ∈ N+. Let Id : R → R be the unary identity gate Id( t) def.

> 0

49 Proof of Lemma A.15. We first create “levels” of nodes. Since G is a DAG with roots Vin and a unique terminal node vout , we define a level function ℓ : V → { 0, 1, . . . , L } by 

ℓ(v) def. 

= max length of a directed path from some u ∈ Vin to v .

Then ℓ(u) = 0 for each u ∈ Vin , and ℓ(vout ) = L since the depth of G is L. Moreover, if (u, v ) ∈ E, then every directed path from an input node to u extends to a directed path to v, and therefore 

ℓ(v) ≥ ℓ(u) + 1 .

> 0

= Vin and V ⋆L = {vout }. Moreover, the longest directed path still has length 

L (we only subdivided edges), so the depth remains L.Since each subdivided edge (u, v ) has been replaced by a directed path whose internal nodes apply Id , then the value transmitted from u to v is unchanged. Since all original gates are preserved, the output at vout is unchanged. Hence fC⋆ = f C .It remains only to tally the number of added nodes and edges. For an original edge (u, v ) ∈ E with 

d = ℓ(v) − ℓ(u) we replaced one edge by exactly d edges, i.e., we added d − 1 edges. Therefore 

|E⋆| = |E| + X

> (u,v )∈E

(d − 1) = |E| + X

> (u,v )∈E

 ℓ(v) − ℓ(u) − 1 ≤ | E| + ( L − 1) |E| ≤ L|E|.

Thus |E⋆| = O(L|E|). Finally, each long edge contributes at most one new node to any intermediate layer, so for each fixed l we have |V ⋆l | ≤ | Vl| + |E|. If |Vl| ≤ Υ then |V ⋆l | ≤ Υ + |E|.

A.3.2 Step 2: Layer Alignment via Identity Padding 

We already know that parallelization is possible in our language, namely, if f, g ∈ A then so is [f ⊤, g ⊤]⊤.However, it is less clear that several subnetworks of different depths can be parallelized. In the continuous piecewise linear MLP case (with finitely many pieces), e.g., ReLU , this is well-known to be exactly possible since such networks can exactly compute the identity map; cf. [26, Proposition 5] or [71, Lemma 2.17] for a specialized ReLU MLP case. 50 Computation Nodes Latent Variables 

> Variables

Canonicalized -Circuit (a) The canonicalized G⋆-circuit. Algorithmic Surgery :  

> Approximately Emulating Each Gates
> Approximated
> Computation Gates Latent Variables
> Variables

(b) Surgery: Gates swapped for ANN approximate emulators. 

Figure 7: Algorithmic Surgery of the G-Circuit in Figure 3: Upon canonicalizing the G-circuit computing f ,layer by layer, we replace each node and gate by a small “expert” ANN sub-network approximately performing the same computation, according to the results in Table 6. The result is a circuit whose sub-circuits are each exactly ANNs, and whose totality approximately computes the same function f as the original G-circuit in Figure 6a, with approximately the same complexity (depending only approximately logarithmically on 1/ε ), and with roughly the same internal graph structure as the DAG encoding the order of operations of the original G-circuit. The idea of “algorithmic surgery” builds on the technique of [103, 110].

# B Proofs of Implications I: Minimax-Optimal Approximation 

## B.1 Proof of Optimal Approximation of Uniformly-Continuous Functions

Sup-Norm Case: 

Since X ⊆ Rn is compact, it is closed and bounded by the Heine-Borel theorem; whence there exists some

## B.2 Proof of Optimal Approximation of Besov Functions 

Proof of Theorem 4.4. Since X is an (ϵ, δ )-domain, the main results of [161] imply that there exists a bounded linear extension operator E : Bαp,q (X ) → Bαp,q (Rd). Upon composing the extension operator E with the restriction operator 18 E : Bαp,q (Rd) ∋ f 7 → f |(0 ,1) n ∈ Bαp,q ((0 , 1) n) we obtain an f ↑ ∈ Bαp,q ((0 , 1) n) whose restriction to X coincides with f and such that ∥f ↑∥Bαp,q ((0 ,1) n) ≤ C ∥f ↑∥Bαp,q ((0 ,1) n) for some absolute constant 

C > 0; i.e., their Besov norms differ only by an absolute constant. Thus, we may, without loss of generality, approximate f ↑ on (0 , 1) n and then infer an approximation of f on X by restriction; using the simple fact that ∥ · ∥ Lp(X ) ≤ ∥ · ∥ Lp((0 ,1) n). Thus, for convenience, we may relabel f ↑ by f and argue on the cube (0 , 1) n.Without loss of generality, we may assume that 0 < ε . By the standard spline characterization of Besov spaces on [0 , 1] d, there exist coefficients {βk,ν (f )}k∈N0,ν ∈Λk such that f = P∞

## B.3 Proof of Optimal Approximation of Holomorphic functions 

Before proving Theorem 4.7, which is the main goal of this section, we introduce some technical ingredients regarding polynomial approximation that are needed for the proof. We start by defining orthogonal Legendre polynomials. In particular, we consider the basis (Ψ ν )ν∈Nd 

> 0

of L2([ −1, 1] d)-orthonormal tensorized Legendre polynomials, defined as 

Ψν = O

> i∈supp( ν)

ψνi , ∀ν = ( νi)di=1 ∈ Nd

> 0

, (B.20) where (ψn)∞ 

> n=0

is the one-dimensional L2-orthonormal basis of Legendre polynomials over the interval [−1, 1] ;see, e.g., [4, Section 2.2.2] for an analytic definition of ψn. Here the space L2([ −1, 1]) is equipped with the uniform probability measure (with constant density 1/2) and, analogously, L2([ −1, 1] d) is equipped with the uniform probability measure (having constant density 1/2d). For an introduction to multivariate Legendre polynomials we refer the reader to, e.g., [4, Chapter 2]. Before stating the main auxiliary result, Lemma B.2, we introduce the notion of lower (also known as downward closed ) multi-index set, which will be instrumental in our analysis. Figure 9 provides examples of lower and non-lower multi-index sets of N20.

Definition B.1 (Lower Set) . A multi-index set S ⊆ Nd 

> 0

is called a lower set if the following property holds for every ν, μ ∈ Nd

> 0

:if ν ∈ S and μ ≤ ν, then μ ∈ S. 

Here the inequality is intended componentwise, i.e., μ ≤ ν means that μi ≤ νi for every i ∈ [d].A useful property of lower sets that will be used later is that for every ν ∈ S, the box Qdi=1 [0 , ν i] ∩ Nd 

> 0

is contained in S (see Figure 9). We are now ready to state the main approximation lemma. The version stated here corresponds to [4, Theorem 3.15] (with the choice ϵ = 1 ), based on a result from [144]. 64 Lemma B.2 (Exponential Rates via Legendre Polynomials) . Let d ∈ N, γ > 0 and f ∈ H d,γ from Defini-tion 4.6. Then, for every s ∈ N large enough, there exists a lower set S ⊆ Nd 

> 0

of cardinality |S| ≤ s and a coefficient vector (cν )ν∈S such that

≤ exp( −γs 1/d ), (B.21) 

where (Ψ ν )ν∈Nd 

> 0

are the L2([ −1, 1] d)-orthogonal tensorized Legendre polynomials defined in (B.20) .

# C Proof of Implications II: Simulation of Numerical Schemes 

## C.1 ODE Flow Solvers 

Our proof of Proposition 5.5 will take a detour to first show that quadrature methods for ODEs can be efficiently encoded. 

C.1.1 Quadrature Method Emulators for Low-Regularity Integrands 

We now show that any ANN can efficiently approximate quadrature rules for integral approximation. In many cases, this yields better rates than those guaranteed by direct minimax-optimal approximation of the antiderivative, when only its regularity is known (e.g., Lipschitz or Cs, 1 for some s ∈ N+).

# D Proof of Classical TCS Computation Result - Corollary 5.1 

Proof of Corollary 5.1. Let n def. 

= |Ek|. We first observe that Algorithm 1 computes DW 

> 1,2

exactly on (0 , ∞)Ek .Indeed, after the initialization step, dj is the minimum weight of a path from 1 to j using at most one edge. Suppose inductively that, after r − 1 edge-relaxation rounds, dj is the minimum weight of a path from 1 to j

using at most r − 1 edges. Then the update 

edj = min 

n

dj , min 

> q∈[k]+\{ 1,j }

 dq + W{q,j }

o

takes the minimum over all paths from 1 to j using at most r edges: either the path already uses at most 

r − 1 edges, or its last edge is {q, j } and its initial segment is a path from 1 to q using at most r − 1 edges. Thus, after the final round, dj is the minimum weight of all paths from 1 to j using at most k − 1 edges. Since all edge weights are positive, every shortest path is simple and therefore uses at most k − 1 edges. Hence the returned value is d2 = DW

> 1,2

.We encode Algorithm 1 as a G2,1 

> t-alg

-circuit. The additions dq +W{q,j } are binary addition gates. The binary minimum gate belongs to the algebro-tropical language since min {x, y } = − max {− x, −y}, using only affine operations and the binary maximum gate. Each inner minimum over q ∈ [k]+ \ { 1, j } is implemented by a balanced binary tree of depth O(log k) and size O(k). In each relaxation round there are O(k2) addition and binary minimum gates, and there are O(k) rounds. Therefore the resulting binary algebro-tropical circuit has 70 ∆k = O(k log k), Nk = O(k3), and Υk = O(k2). Moreover, since every gate has arity at most two, the number of circuit edges satisfies |ECk | = O(Nk) = O(k3). Hence, in the notation of Theorem 3.2, 

¯Υk = max {1, Υk + |ECk |} = O(k3).

Let Fk : REk → R denote the function computed by this finite tropical circuit, i.e., the output obtained by running the same Bellman-Ford-Moore recursion for arbitrary real edge-weights. The circuit computes Fk

exactly on [−1, 1] Ek , and the preceding paragraph shows that Fk(W ) = DW 

> 1,2

for every W ∈ [w−, 1] Ek .Apply the pure min-plus specialization of the proof of Theorem 3.2 to the exact G2,1 

> t-alg

-circuit computing 

Fk, with accuracy parameter η def. 

= ε/ 3. Since the circuit computes Fk exactly, the specialization produces an 

ANN Φdist:1 ,2 : R|Ek | → R such that 

sup  

> W∈[−1,1] Ek

Fk(W ) − Φdist:1 ,2(W ) ≤ 2η < ε. 

Restricting this estimate to [w−, 1] Ek and using Fk(W ) = DW 

> 1,2

on this positive cube gives the required approximation bound. It remains only to read off the complexity estimates. We use here the pure min-plus specialization of the proof of Theorem 3.2. In this circuit, the only non-affine gates are binary minima, equivalently binary maxima after affine sign changes. The additions are affine operations in the network wiring and therefore introduce no gate-emulation error. Moreover, the Bellman-Ford-Moore recursion propagates errors additively: if, at the beginning of a relaxation round, all current labels dj are approximated within ρ, and if each binary-minimum tree is approximated within accuracy τ at each of its O(log k) levels, then after the round all updated labels are approximated within ρ + O(τ log k). Since there are O(k) rounds, choosing τ def. 

= c0 ηk log k with c0 > 0 small enough yields a total circuit-emulation error at most η.By Proposition A.11, each binary maximum/minimum gate can be emulated to accuracy τ with depth and number of non-zero parameters O  log(1 /τ ) log ◦2(1 /τ ). The binary-minimum trees have depth O(log k), and the Bellman-Ford-Moore circuit has O(k) sequential relaxation rounds. Hence the resulting ANN has depth

# E Proof of Theorem 3.1 

Our proof of Theorem 3.1 is divided into three cases: affine non-linearities (where universal approximation fails), piecewise linear (which is reducible to the ReLU -MLP case where universality is already well-established with minimax-rates), and the remaining definable case which we have already taken care of in Theorem 4.1 when μ is the Lebesgue measure on [0 , 1] d.

## E.1 Case 1: The Affine Case – When Universal Approximation Fails 

Lemma E.1. Fix 1 ≤ p < ∞. If A consists only of affine functions, then there exists some ε > 0 and some 

gε ∈ C([0 , 1] d) such that: there exists no ANN ˆf : Rd → R satisfying ∥gε − ˆf ∥Lp([0 ,1] d) ≤ ε.

71 Proof. Let Aff d

> def.

= {x 7 → a⊤x + b : a ∈ Rd, b ∈ R}. Since every element of A is affine in its input variable, it follows by induction over the layers that every realization of an ANN is itself an affine map. Hence every 

ANN ˆf : Rd → R belongs to Aff d.Now view Aff d as a subspace of Lp([0 , 1] d). Since dim(Aff d) = d + 1 < ∞, this is a finite-dimensional linear subspace, and therefore it is closed in Lp([0 , 1] d). Hence Aff d cannot be dense in Lp([0 , 1] d), since no finite-dimensional linear subspace of an infinite-dimensional Banach space can be dense therein. Therefore, there exists some gε ∈ C([0 , 1] d) such that gε /∈ Aff dLp([0 ,1] d). Setting ε def. 

= 12 inf h∈Aff d ∥gε − h∥Lp([0 ,1] d), we have ε > 0, and no ANN ˆf : Rd → R can satisfy ∥gε − ˆf ∥Lp([0 ,1] d) ≤ ε.

## E.2 Case 2: The Piecewise Linear Case 

As the next simple result shows, it is reducible to the ReLU MLP case where virtually all these approximation guarantees are known (up to a log(1 /ε )-factor slower for multiplication and faster for maximization). 

Proposition E.2 (Reduction to ReLU -MLP Setting) . Let f : Rd → R be continuous and piecewise linear in the sense of Definition 2.3. If f is not affine, then there exists a non-constant affine map E : R → Rd such that, for every M > 0, there exists a (f ◦ E)-MLP ˆfReLU ,M : R → R of depth 1 and width 2 such that: for all 

x ∈ [−M, M ]ReLU( x) = ˆfReLU ,M (x).

Proof of Proposition E.2. Let E(t) = x0 + tv with v̸ = 0 . Since f is piecewise linear, for any partition 

{Π(p)}Pp=1 as in Definition 2.3, the sets E−1(Π (p)) ⊆ R form a definable partition of R, and on each of them 

(f ◦ E)( t) = A(p)(x0 + tv ) + b(p) is affine in t. Hence f ◦ E is piecewise linear. We now show that one can choose E so that f ◦ E is not affine. Assume by contradiction that f ◦ E is affine for every affine map 

E : R → Rd. Fix x, y ∈ Rd and define Ex,y (t) def. 

= x + t(y − x). Then t 7 → f (Ex,y (t)) is affine, so for every 

λ ∈ R, f ((1 − λ)x + λy ) = (1 − λ)f (x) + λf (y). Thus f is affine on Rd, contradicting the assumption; whence there is an affine map E such that f ◦ E is not affine. Since f ◦ E is piecewise linear, it must then have at least two pieces. Applying [203, Proposition 1 (b)] with ρ def. 

= f ◦ E and D def. 

= [ −M, M ], we find that for every M > 0 there exists a (f ◦ E)-MLP ˆfReLU ,M : R → R satisfying ˆfReLU ,M (x) = ReLU( x) for all x ∈ [−M, M ]. Moreover, 

ˆfReLU ,M has depth 1 and width 2.The immediate consequence of Proposition E.2 and a (minimax-optimal) universal approximation theorem for ReLU -MLPs, e.g., [204, 148, 173, 81], is that ANNs with non-affine (continuous) piecewise linear non-linearities A are universal approximators. 

Corollary E.3 (Universality of ANNs with Piecewise Linear Non-Linearities) . If A contains a continuous piecewise linear but non-affine non-linearity σ : Rm → R, then for every uniformly continuous function 

g : [0 , 1] d → R, every modulus of regularity ω of g, every 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ , and every ε > 0, there exists an ANN 

ˆgε : [0 , 1] d → R satisfying 

∥g − ˆgε∥Lp([0 ,1] d) ≤ ω(ε).

Moreover, the width and size of ˆgε are O(ε−d), while its depth is O(1) .Proof of Corollary E.3. Let σ ∈ A be a continuous piecewise linear, non-affine non-linearity. Fix a uniformly continuous function g : [0 , 1] d → R, 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ , and ε > 0. Let ω be a modulus of regularity of g on [0 , 1] d.Choose nε

> def.

= max {1, ⌈ d 

> 2ε

⌉} . Then d 

> 2nε

≤ ε. Hence, by [81, Theorem 4.1], there exists a ReLU -MLP Φε on 

[0 , 1] d with width at most 8d(nε +1) d, depth at most ⌈log 2 d⌉+4 , and at most 16 d(nε +1) d nonzero parameters, such that 

∥g − Φε∥Lp([0 ,1] d) ≤ ∥ g − Φε∥L∞([0 ,1] d) ≤ ω

 d

2nε



≤ ω(ε). (E.1) Since Φε is a finite network and [0 , 1] d is compact, there exists Mε > 0 such that every scalar pre-activation appearing in Φε lies in [−Mε, M ε] whenever the input belongs to [0 , 1] d.72 Applying Proposition E.2 to the non-affine piecewise linear map σ, we obtain a non-constant affine map 

E : R → Rm such that there exists an (σ ◦ E)-MLP ˆfReLU ,M ε : R → R of depth 1 and width 2 satisfying 

ˆfReLU ,M ε (x) = ReLU( x)

for all x ∈ [−Mε, M ε]. Since σ ∈ A , this depth-1, width-2 realization is an ANN block. We now form an ANN ˆgε by replacing each ReLU activation in Φε by a copy of this fixed ANN block. By the choice of Mε, every such replacement is exact on [0 , 1] d, and hence 

ˆgε(x) = Φ ε(x)

for all x ∈ [0 , 1] d. Therefore, by (E.1) we have ∥g − ˆgε∥Lp([0 ,1] d) = ∥g − Φε∥Lp([0 ,1] d) ≤ ω(ε). Finally, since 

ˆfReLU ,M ε has O(1) depth, width, and size, replacing each ReLU gate in Φε by this block changes the width and size by at most a constant factor and the depth by at most a constant factor. Thus ˆgε has width and size 

O(( nε + 1) d) = O(ε−d), while its depth remains O(1) .

## E.3 Case 3: The Non-Piecewise Definable Case 

Proof of Theorem 3.1. If A consists only of affine functions, then Lemma E.1 implies that there exists some 

ε > 0 and some gε ∈ C([0 , 1] d) such that no ANN fε : Rd → R satisfies ∥gε − fε∥Lp([0 ,1] d) ≤ ε. Since 

∥h∥Lp([0 ,1] d) ≤ ∥ h∥L∞([0 ,1] d) for every h ∈ L∞([0 , 1] d), it follows a fortiori that no such ANN can satisfy (3.1). Conversely, if A has at least one non-affine function f then there are two sub-cases to be considered. Either (A) f is non-affine and piecewise linear, in which case the conclusion follows from Corollary E.3, or (B) f is not piecewise linear, in which case the conclusion follows from Theorem 4.1. 

# F Notation 

The following is a full list of the notation used in our paper. 

Notation. We use the following notation throughout the paper. (i) N+ denotes the positive integers, and N0 denotes the nonnegative integers. (ii) Z, Q, R, and C denote the integers, rationals, reals, and complex numbers, respectively. (iii) For N ∈ N+, [N ]+ denotes the set {1, . . . , N }.(iv) For m ∈ N+, Id Rm denotes the identity map on Rm.(v) For 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ , Ball p(x, r ) denotes the closed ℓp-ball centered at x with radius r > 0.(vi) A denotes a dictionary of nonlinearities, and an ANN denotes a neural network built from nonlinearities in A.(vii) If f : Rn × Rp → Rm belongs to A, then fθ

> def.

= f (·, θ ) denotes the corresponding parameter-frozen map. (viii) Θ = ( θl, A l, b l)Ll=1 denotes the full parameter vector of an ANN, where θl are local nonlinear parameters, 

Al are affine connection matrices, and bl are bias vectors. (ix) F[f ] def. 

= {fΘ(·) : Θ ∈ RP } denotes the function class realized by the parametric network f .(x) f ∥k denotes the k-fold parallelization of a nonlinearity f .(xi) f ◦k denotes the 2 ≤ k-fold composition of f with itself, for any self-map f .(xii) S denotes a fixed o-minimal structure over the real field, and “definable” always means definable in S.

73 (xiii) G denotes a dictionary of gates, i.e., elementary operations allowed in a circuit. (xiv) [Rn : R] denotes the set of all functions from Rn to R.(xv) Gk,c alg denotes the algebraic gate dictionary, containing constants, identity gates, addition gates, and multiplication gates, with arity at most k and constants bounded by c.(xvi) Gk,c t-alg denotes the algebro-tropical gate dictionary, obtained by adding absolute value and maximization gates to Gk,c alg .(xvii) Gk,c,r ⋆ 

> rt -alg

denotes the radical-algebro-tropical gate dictionary, obtained by adding integer powers and radicals of order at most r⋆.(xviii) Gk,c,r ⋆ 

> Rat

denotes the rational-tropical gate dictionary, obtained by adding the inversion gate x 7 → 1/x .(xix) D = ( V, E ) denotes a directed acyclic graph, with vertex set V and directed edge set E.(xx) For a node v ∈ V , pa v denotes the set of parents of v, and ch v denotes the set of children of v.(xxi) Vin , Vout , and Vcomp denote the input nodes, output nodes, and computation nodes of a DAG, respectively. (xxii) DAG d,D denotes the class of connected DAGs with d input nodes, D output nodes, and finitely many computation nodes. (xxiii) A G-circuit is a triple C def. 

= ( V, E, G), where D = ( V, E ) is a DAG and G def. 

= {gv }v∈V is a family of gates decorating its vertices. (xxiv) f C denotes the function represented, or computed, by the circuit C.(xxv) x(v) denotes the value carried by the node v during the recursive evaluation of a circuit. (xxvi) Π denotes a lifting channel, used to duplicate or route the input variables before circuit evaluation. (xxvii) ∆, Υ, and N denote circuit depth, circuit width, and circuit size, respectively. (xxviii) G∆,Υ 

> d0

denotes the class of functions computable by G-circuits with lifting dimension d0, depth ∆, and width Υ.(xxix) ΦG denotes the G-propagator, which controls the growth of bounded boxes under the gates in G.(xxx) RG denotes the G-regulator, which controls the worst-case modulus of continuity of gates in G.(xxxi) HG,A denotes the (G, A)-hardness map, which records the cost of emulating gates from G by ANNs. (xxxii) HG,A(M, ε ) = (∆ M,ε , ΥM,ε , N M,ε ) denotes the depth, width, and size required to approximate a gate on 

[−M, M ]dg to accuracy ε.(xxxiii) δ· = ( δl)∆
